Sensitive banking and identity details have been redacted.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=hXXeoSeS","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eFrom one attack to a wider campaign\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe controlled interaction gave us visibility into the live fraud attempt, while the forged NDA gave us something else: a set of document fingerprints we could hunt for.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eUsing its structure, wording and embedded identifiers, the team found four additional NDA samples connected to other targeted individuals.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe organizations and professions varied. The targets included senior people in private equity, industrial finance, sales, mining and energy. For each of them, an acquisition or strategic investment narrative would have been credible enough to justify initial engagement.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe advisers also changed. Some documents impersonated PwC, while others used KPMG or Ogier branding. There is no indication that any of these firms were compromised or involved in the operation. Their names and identities were being abused to make the documents appear legitimate.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDespite the different branding, the documents followed substantially the same sequence of sections and reused the same legal language. They all imposed confidentiality, directed communications towards WhatsApp and personal email, and introduced a short period between the NDA date and the supposed public announcement.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThey also contained the same unusual numeric identifier across documents attributed to different firms and prepared for unrelated recipients. This kind of residue is common when criminals reuse document templates. Names, dates, logos and transaction references may be changed, while less visible elements remain untouched.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe repeated structure suggests that the attackers were not building each operation from scratch. They had developed a reusable M\u0026amp;A fraud package that could be adapted to different people and companies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the Gen case, we observed the complete chain, from WhatsApp contact to payment request and demand for MT103 confirmation. For the other four targets, we found the malicious NDA documents carrying the same core narrative and document fingerprints, but did not observe the later payment stage.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTaken together, the evidence points to a wider M\u0026amp;A-themed fraud campaign built around the same narrative and document toolkit. It does not establish that every target received identical payment instructions or that every document was operated by the same individual.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eNo malware, no compromised mailbox\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePhantom Deal\u003c/strong\u003e did not require an exploit, malicious attachment or stolen corporate email account.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe operation drew on publicly available information, real names, photographs, company history and familiar business procedures. WhatsApp made the contact immediate, the forged NDA justified secrecy, and the acquisition narrative created the urgency needed to move the victim towards payment. The financial request appeared only after the surrounding story had been established.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA security team searching only for malware or suspicious email links could miss the entire operation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attack was designed to induce a process failure rather than exploit a technical vulnerability. The criminals attempted to convince the target that bypassing normal controls was necessary to protect a confidential deal.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe warning signs appeared well before the Hong Kong bank account. Keeping a sensitive corporate transaction on WhatsApp and personal email was already enough to require independent verification.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow companies can interrupt the chain\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eConfidential transactions require restricted access, but secrecy should never remove independent verification.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations can reduce exposure by establishing a small number of rules that apply even to the most sensitive acquisitions, investments and restructuring projects:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e79a03ebdcc50053e9752b64215ac6d0d\"\u003ePayment instructions must be verified through an independently established channel, not through contact details supplied during the transaction.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e56458281a15b902c959078fd579c2999\"\u003eNo executive, adviser or board member should be able to override payment controls through WhatsApp, personal email or a confidentiality claim.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e18c724391af3cb0258190c8ed3abd5c9\"\u003eLegal, Finance, Treasury and Corporate Development teams should have a defined escalation route for unexpected transaction requests.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e14239b646035ac07ac66d03bc0296926\"\u003eStaff involved in payments should be trained to recognize requests for MT103 documents, UETR references and other confirmation data as part of the fraud chain, not merely as administrative follow-up.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea23d5cfe13818e8eefd6ca3cdb93d591\"\u003eExternal advisers should be contacted using verified directory information whenever their identity or instructions are in doubt.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli class=\"ck-list-marker-bold\" data-list-item-id=\"edfd65aefb0bda3b6fe5bc0523c729c49\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eChanges of communication channel, especially from corporate systems to private accounts, should trigger additional scrutiny.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe same rules must apply when the request appears to come from the CEO, a board member or someone already known to the recipient.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAn NDA can limit who is told about a transaction. It should never prevent the transaction from being authenticated.\u003c/h3\u003e\u003ch2\u003eReal details, false transaction\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePhantom Deal\u003c/strong\u003e drew credibility from genuine information. The people, companies and advisers were real, as was the acquisition history connecting Gen and Avast. International payments, professional retainers and intercompany receivables are also normal elements of large corporate transactions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe transaction tying those details together was fabricated, and the execution was uneven. The caller’s voice did not match the colleague being impersonated, while the explanation for the payment contained inconsistencies that a lawyer familiar with the company’s processes could identify quickly. A more coherent story might have been considerably more dangerous.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhy try this against a security company? Probably for the same reason they try it anywhere else: a security company is still a company. It has money, confidential transactions and employees whose jobs do not involve analysing scams. The attackers were not trying to defeat our security technology. They were trying to find one person who might accept the story and step outside the normal process.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn this case, they chose someone who knew the colleague, recognized that the voice did not match and understood that the payment explanation made no sense. From that moment, the supposed victim was no longer following their script. He was helping us investigate it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is also some irony in how it ended. After spending days building a narrative around trust, secrecy and urgency, someone connected to the fraud operation repeatedly interacted with the fake payment confirmation we placed in front of them.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe NDA was intended to keep the operation confidential. Its reused language, structure and identifiers ultimately helped expose the campaign behind it.\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"14","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-09/Hero%20image.jpg?h=745bb08b\u0026itok=apuJoCf8","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/free-ai-at-the-price-of-your-prompt","title":"Free AI, at the Price of Your Prompt","subtitle":"You choose a model, ask a question and get an answer. Every call to \u003ccode\u003eNtTraceEvent\u003c/code\u003e then raises an \u003ccode\u003eEXCEPTION_SINGLE_STEP\u003c/code\u003e exception, which the handler resolves by pointing the instruction pointer (\u003ccode\u003eEIP\u003c/code\u003e) at a stub that does nothing but return zero, and resuming execution there. Execution therefore never reaches the body of \u003ccode\u003eNtTraceEvent\u003c/code\u003e, so the event is never logged, and since the call still returns \u003ccode\u003eSTATUS_SUCCESS\u003c/code\u003e (\u003ccode\u003e0x00000000\u003c/code\u003e), the caller has no indication anything went wrong.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_6.jpg.webp?itok=ghu7MZdA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_6.jpg.webp?itok=clBKPb9k 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":400},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_6.jpg.webp?itok=Tm0JJCyb 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_6.jpg.webp?itok=V7wA7kHe 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":304},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_6.jpg.webp?itok=XlIEAuxo 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_6.jpg.webp?itok=nCOd_sR2 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":236}],"caption":"Figure 6: WordlistLoader setting up a hardware breakpoint on ntdll!NtTraceEvent. The mapping from English words to byte values is defined by a build-specific wordlist of 256 distinct words: the word at index 0 decodes to \u003ccode\u003e0x00\u003c/code\u003e, the word at index 144 to \u003ccode\u003e0x90\u003c/code\u003e, and so on.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBoth the wordlist and the encoded sequence are stored as arrays of pointers, so the loader matches addresses rather than the strings themselves. Rebuilding the shellcode thus comes down to iterating over the word sequence, looking up each word in the list, and writing the index of the matching entry into the output buffer. Once the entire sequence has been processed, the buffer contains the reconstructed shellcode. Hence the name WordlistLoader.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_8.jpg.webp?itok=vBOyyjes 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_8.jpg.webp?itok=B_LdZLZN 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":539},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_8.jpg.webp?itok=2G4m6F0Y 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_8.jpg.webp?itok=WdnMS8sH 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":410},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_8.jpg.webp?itok=ZMPbFyde 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_8.jpg.webp?itok=XXHaZsx0 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":318}],"caption":"Figure 8: WordlistLoader decoding the shellcode. Using the standard \u003ccode\u003ecall/pop\u003c/code\u003e trick, the stub takes its own return address as the patching target and enters an outer loop of 254 rounds, with each iteration first executing an inner delay loop and then patching the target address (which is the same for every iteration). The value written decreases with every round, ultimately reaching \u003ccode\u003e0x90\u003c/code\u003e on the final iteration. As shown in Figure 11 below, the patched byte is part of a \u003ccode\u003ecall dword ptr [eax-0x6F6F6F70]\u003c/code\u003e instruction, which corresponds to the \u003ccode\u003eFF 90 90 90 90 90\u003c/code\u003e opcodes. ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_15.jpg.webp?itok=Cs5qFkOE","link":""}]},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003cp\u003eAnother notable change concerns API hashing. In version 4.0.2 Beta, all imports were resolved using a single hashing algorithm, so precomputing the hashes once was sufficient to annotate every call. In newer versions, the resolution is spread across dozens of nearly identical resolvers, each using its own multiply-rotate-XOR hash with different constants and operation order. Consequently, the same API name maps to a different hash value depending on which resolver handles the call. Hashes must therefore be precomputed separately for each resolver, slowing down the analysis.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFinally, as is quite typical for Amatera, it has revised its string obfuscation once again. Each string is now stored as a ciphertext blob, with its length and a numeric key index supplied at the decryption call site. For every byte, the key index and the byte's offset are mixed into a 64-bit state through four splitmix64-style rounds, producing four key bytes. The plaintext byte is then recovered as \u003ccode\u003eROR((ciphertext ^ key_3) - key_2, key_1) ^ key_0\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eInterestingly, the decryption routine also employs a deliberate stack modification aimed at thwarting decompilation. The modification occurs in a dead branch guarded by a flag that is never set anywhere in the binary. Should the branch ever be taken, it would subtract \u003ccode\u003e0x7FFFFF00\u003c/code\u003e from \u003ccode\u003eESP\u003c/code\u003e, call a stub that does nothing but return zero, and add the same value back to \u003ccode\u003eESP\u003c/code\u003e. Such a stack shift (of almost 2 GB) is nothing a regular compiler would ever emit, and is enough to break IDA's stack-frame reconstruction and make it refuse to decompile the function. For now, we have only observed this trick in a single function, but given the pace at which Amatera evolves, it would not be surprising to see it spread across the whole binary in the future.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=hAK4U6K4 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=MvJp_utg 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":507},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=cEooJJEG 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=HwVDJBXH 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":385},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=qmXz9Uu5 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=MSpVWJlT 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":299}],"caption":"Figure 16: Amatera’s stack manipulation. Second, it is worth highlighting that Amatera is a 32-bit stealer, as that is what makes the techniques described below possible.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn a 32-bit process running on 64-bit Windows, every interaction with the operating system has to eventually end in the 64-bit kernel. From the process's point of view, however, everything has to look as if it were running on 32-bit Windows. That is where the WoW64 (Windows on Windows) layer comes into play, seamlessly translating WinAPI calls from 32-bit user space to the 64-bit operating system kernel. Simply put, the whole mechanism is built around the fact that the process runs with two ntdlls loaded side by side: the 32-bit one the application interacts with, which \"forwards\" the call to the WoW64 layer, and the 64-bit one, used once the call reaches 64-bit mode.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn practice, the application calls a function in the 32-bit ntdll, which passes control to \u003ccode\u003entdll!Wow64SystemServiceCall\u003c/code\u003e. From there, the execution continues through \u003ccode\u003entdll!Wow64Transition\u003c/code\u003e and lands in \u003ccode\u003ewow64cpu!KiFastSystemCall\u003c/code\u003e, the stub through which the WoW64 layer leaves the 32-bit world. The transition is achieved by a far jump using the \u003ccode\u003e0x33\u003c/code\u003e selector, which switches the processor into 64-bit mode, where the WoW64 layer issues the actual syscall.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn an effort to be stealthier and evade user-mode hooks, Amatera skips the 32-bit ntdll part of that chain altogether, resolving the syscall numbers on its own and entering the WoW64 layer directly through \u003ccode\u003ewow64cpu!KiFastSystemCall\u003c/code\u003e. The transition itself is performed as an indirect call to \u003ccode\u003eTEB-\u0026gt;WOW32Reserved\u003c/code\u003e, a field that conveniently holds the address of \u003ccode\u003ewow64cpu!KiFastSystemCall\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn version 4.0.2 Beta, Amatera had a dedicated stub for every syscall it wanted to invoke this way, each of them loading the masked SSN from a global, unmasking it with a XOR, and calling \u003ccode\u003eTEB-\u0026gt;WOW32Reserved\u003c/code\u003e directly through the \u003ccode\u003ecall large dword ptr fs:0C0h\u003c/code\u003e instruction. However, regular applications do not make direct calls to \u003ccode\u003eTEB-\u0026gt;WOW32Reserved\u003c/code\u003e, making the sequence fairly noisy.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=LXPyWpod 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=sL2VsgNc 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":206},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=S_PqSahI 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=X0ZE0QwH 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":157},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=iElfyxz4 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=wt2O3GcZ 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":121}],"caption":"Figure 17: Example of a WoW64 syscall gate in Amatera 4.0.2 Beta. Reaching into a 64-bit process requires working with 64-bit pointers, which do not fit through the WoW64 gate, and while the 32-bit ntdll exposes a handful of \u003ccode\u003eNtWow64*\u003c/code\u003e helpers for exactly that purpose (such as, for example, \u003ccode\u003eNtWow64AllocateVirtualMemory64\u003c/code\u003e or \u003ccode\u003eNtWow64WriteVirtualMemory64\u003c/code\u003e), they do not cover everything Amatera requires for its injection technique.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThat is why Amatera employs Heaven's Gate, a technique that allows 64-bit code to be executed from within a 32-bit process on a 64-bit operating system (essentially, a manual version of the mode switch the WoW64 layer performs internally, naturally skipping the 32-bit ntdll layer as well). Heaven's Gate, as such, however, is nothing new in Amatera and has been there for quite some time already. What has changed is what the Gate leads to for a part of the calls Amatera makes through it.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn version 4.0.2 Beta, Amatera used Heaven's Gate to invoke 64-bit ntdll exported functions, passing the arguments according to the standard Microsoft x64 calling convention, with the execution therefore going through the export's prologue. However, just as AV and EDR products can hook exported functions in the 32-bit ntdll, they can hook the 64-bit ones as well. To avoid that, in newer versions, a fixed set of twelve syscalls (for example, \u003ccode\u003eNtReadVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eNtWriteVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eNtAllocateVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eNtMapViewOfSection\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003eNtSetIoCompletion\u003c/code\u003e) is set up to skip the export entirely, with Amatera resolving their syscall numbers on its own (and validating them against a pristine copy of ntdll read from disk) and issuing each call through an indirect x64 syscall trampoline built at runtime.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eEach trampoline consists of a 24-byte stub containing \u003ccode\u003emov r10, rcx\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003emov eax, SSN\u003c/code\u003e, and a RIP-relative indirect jump to a \u003ccode\u003esyscall; ret\u003c/code\u003e gadget inside ntdll (located by scanning the ntdll mapped in memory). To build and execute them, Amatera creates a section with \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateSection\u003c/code\u003e and maps it twice in its own process using \u003ccode\u003eNtMapViewOfSection\u003c/code\u003e: once with \u003ccode\u003eRW\u003c/code\u003e permissions for writing the stubs and once with \u003ccode\u003eRX\u003c/code\u003e permissions for running them.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=MXosRfAS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=6sRY9eYX 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":605},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=sJMtHBoM 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=dV79DGOG 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":460},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=gxquwPLu 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=lBYkFjGC 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":357}],"caption":"Figure 21: Amatera building the x64 syscall trampoline. In fact, the bypass is so alike that we believe Amatera's implementation was directly inspired by it. Therefore, as we have already covered the technique in \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\"\u003eour previous blog post\u003c/a\u003e, we will only do a quick recap of the bypass's main idea and focus solely on where Amatera's approach differs. For interested readers, we recommend revisiting our earlier post.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFrom a high-level perspective, the bypass relies on the fact that a running Chromium-based browser keeps the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e stored in memory. There, however, the key is protected with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e using the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag, which limits decryption to the process that encrypted the data. Therefore, the bypass comes down to locating the protected key and injecting a payload that decrypts it using the complementary \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e from within the browser's context.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eTo find the key, Amatera, just like Remus and Lumma, scans the browser's memory for a 20-byte pattern, trying to find the \u003ccode\u003elea\u003c/code\u003e instruction that loads the address of the \u003ccode\u003eos_crypt_async::Encryptor\u003c/code\u003e vftable, as that is the class holding the protected \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e. On a match, it resolves the vftable address using the displacement of the \u003ccode\u003elea\u003c/code\u003e instruction, and since a pointer to it sits at the very beginning of every instance of that class, a second scan of the browser's memory makes it possible to locate a live instance holding the key. From there, extracting the protected \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e is just a matter of walking the object's tree of key entries and taking the one tagged \u003ccode\u003ev20\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe clearest evidence of the shared origin, however, is the signature itself. Amatera builds the pattern as \u003ccode\u003e48 8D 05 00 00 00 00 48 89 01 48 8B 02 48 89 41 00 48 8D 41\u003c/code\u003e and pairs it with the wildcard mask \u003ccode\u003e0xEFF87\u003c/code\u003e, the very same mask Remus applies to \u003ccode\u003e48 8D 05 8B CC 8D 02 48 89 01 48 8B 02 48 89 41 5B 48 8D 41\u003c/code\u003e. Breaking it down, the only positions in which the two patterns differ are the four displacement bytes of the \u003ccode\u003elea\u003c/code\u003e instruction and the displacement byte of the \u003ccode\u003emov\u003c/code\u003e instruction. These are precisely the five bytes the mask excludes from the comparison, which Amatera leaves zeroed out, while Remus keeps the values from whichever \u003ccode\u003echrome.dll\u003c/code\u003e build the pattern was lifted from. Everything else, the mask included, matches byte for byte.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=cTWIggL3 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=1R-iHwTw 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":511},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=enfvkIlA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=Io9S_1U8 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":388},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=_qsKWD1x 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=jFUiFwqa 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":301}],"caption":"Figure 22: Amatera building the scan pattern. Remus and Lumma get by with less than a hundred bytes of shellcode as the payload has a single job: to call \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e on the memory pointing to the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e and copy it into a pre-allocated buffer. Amatera's payload is considerably larger (thousands of bytes), but it also does considerably more. Besides the logic for decrypting the key, the payload carries the entire logic for locating it, as well as the logic for recovering the legacy \u003ccode\u003ev10_master_key\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eTo get the payload into the browser, Amatera creates two sections and maps each twice, once locally and once in the browser. The first section holds the payload, mapped locally as \u003ccode\u003eRW\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003eRX\u003c/code\u003e in the browser, whereas the second carries a configuration blob and the output area for the recovered keys, mapped as \u003ccode\u003eRW\u003c/code\u003e on both sides. The configuration blob holds the pointers to the native and crypto functions used by the payload, the path to the browser's \u003ccode\u003eLocal State\u003c/code\u003e, the address of the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e, and the already mentioned ABE pattern with the mask should the payload need to locate the key again (by default, the key is located by the stealer itself, reading the browser's memory remotely, but the payload can repeat the search on its own as a fallback).\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFinally, Amatera's approach differs in the way the payload is launched. Rather than creating a remote thread, Amatera hijacks the browser's thread pool, using the technique known as PoolParty variant 7 (Remote \u003ccode\u003eTP_DIRECT\u003c/code\u003e Insertion), \u003ca href=\"https://www.safebreach.com/blog/process-injection-using-windows-thread-pools/\"\u003epublished by SafeBreach in 2023\u003c/a\u003e. It enumerates the browser's handles with \u003ccode\u003eNtQueryInformationProcess\u003c/code\u003e, duplicates them with \u003ccode\u003eNtDuplicateObject\u003c/code\u003e, identifies the I/O completion port among them with \u003ccode\u003eNtQueryObject\u003c/code\u003e, and queues a \u003ccode\u003eTP_DIRECT\u003c/code\u003e work item on it with \u003ccode\u003eNtSetIoCompletion\u003c/code\u003e, leaving the execution to one of the browser's own worker threads.
